    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.01, F50.02\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello. Honestly, I'm mostly here because my regular physician—Dr. Evans, I've been seeing him for about twelve years now—he practically insisted I come in and talk to someone. He was getting all worked up at my last checkup, and he keeps telling me that I've gotten way too thin. \n\nAnd, I mean, I suppose I can see what he's talking about if I really look at the facts. I work as a building cleaner, mostly doing the evening shifts at this big office complex, and we have these heavy, standard-issue navy work pants. Lately, I can see them just hanging completely loose on me. I have to pull my belt to the very last notch, and the fabric bunches all up around my waist, which is honestly a real nuisance when I'm trying to bend down and mop the baseboards or empty the lower trash cans. \n\nBut... the strange thing is, even with Dr. Evans fussing over me, and even seeing my clothes bagging like that, when I step on the scale in my bathroom... part of me still feels so relieved when the number is lower. I check it every morning, it's just a habit I have, and when that number drops, it just feels like an accomplishment. It's a comforting feeling, even if everyone else is telling me it's a problem. So, anyway, Dr. Evans wasn't happy with my weight, and he made the referral, and that's why I'm sitting here taking up your time today.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been intentionally trying to lose weight or avoid gaining weight, and if so, how have you been doing that?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... yes, I guess you could say I am. It's not like I'm following some strict diet out of a magazine or anything like that, but I definitely make choices to keep the weight off. I just really, really don't want to gain it back. \n\nIt mostly happens around dinner time. You see, because I work the evening shift at the office building, I have to get all the cooking done in the late afternoon before I leave. My husband works a very physical job—he does contracting work—so he expects a heavy, traditional meal when he gets home. And my mother-in-law lives with us now, too. She moved into our spare room about three years ago after she took a bad fall on her front steps and broke her hip. You know how it is at that age, she just never quite bounced back, so she needs a lot of looking after. She likes her comfort foods, things like meatloaf or casseroles, soft things that are easy for her to chew. \n\nSo, I spend a good hour or two in the kitchen every afternoon, and I cook these big, full plates for my husband and my mother-in-law. I make sure they have their meat, their starches, everything they like. But... well, by the time I actually sit down at the table with them, I just look at all that heavy food and I usually tell myself that just a cup of coffee, maybe a few steamed vegetables, or just half a piece of toast is enough for me. \n\nI just sit there with my coffee and my little half-slice of toast, and I watch them eat, and I just tell myself I don't need all those calories. Plus, I always rationalize it by thinking about how I don't want to feel all weighed down and sluggish while I'm pushing that heavy mop bucket around the offices later. It's just easier to keep things very, very light. So yes, I am doing it on purpose, just keeping my intake as small as I possibly can manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you are already underweight?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I mean, yes, it's... it's a very strong fear. It's almost like a panic sometimes, to be honest. Dr. Evans says I'm underweight, and I hear him, I really do, but in my head, the thought of putting any weight back on is just terrifying. \n\nFor example, if I do happen to eat something heavier—like last Sunday, my mother-in-law really wanted me to make these buttermilk biscuits she loves, the kind from scratch with all the butter cut in. And I had one. Just one! But the minute I swallowed it, my stomach just tied itself in knots. Or if I have even a small scoop of pasta, it's the same thing. I spend the entire rest of the day just completely scared that it's going to show on me immediately. \n\nI'll go into the bathroom at work—the ones on the third floor have these big, full-length mirrors that I have to polish anyway—and I just keep checking myself in the mirror. I'll turn sideways, checking my stomach, checking my thighs, just terrified that I can already see that biscuit or that pasta clinging to me. And then my mind just starts racing, thinking about how I need to make up for it. I'll tell myself I need to scrub the floors a little harder, or maybe take the stairs instead of the elevator when I move my cart between floors, or just not eat anything at all the next day. It's just this constant, nagging dread that I'm going to get fat, even though everyone keeps telling me I'm too thin. It's exhausting, really, but I just can't shake that fear. It just sits right there in the back of my mind, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you see yourself as overweight or gaining weight even when others tell you that you're too thin or underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I suppose. I know what Dr. Evans says, and I know what my husband says. But when I look in the mirror—especially those big mirrors on the third floor I mentioned, the ones I have to Windex every Tuesday and Thursday—I just... I don't see what they see. \n\nI look at myself, and I just focus right in on my stomach or my thighs. And if I feel like my stomach looks even the slightest bit full, or if it isn't perfectly flat, I just feel this overwhelming sense of failure. It’s like, if I can just stay small, you know? If I can just stay neat and contained, then I feel like I'm handling everything right. I'm keeping the house clean, I'm taking care of my mother-in-law, I'm doing my job at the offices... it all feels manageable as long as I stay small. \n\nBut the second I look in the mirror and think I look a little heavier, or if my stomach sticks out even a fraction of an inch, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It’s a terrible feeling, just awful. It makes me feel sloppy and out of control, like I'm failing at the one thing I actually have a handle on. So, even though they tell me I'm too thin, when I look at myself, I just see all these areas that still need work. I see the parts of me that aren't perfectly neat and small, and it just makes me feel like I have to try harder. I just can't see this \"too thin\" person they keep talking about. I just see someone who is one bad meal away from losing control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your perception of your body weight or shape influences how you feel about yourself, perhaps even more than other people might expect?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, I would say that. It's... it's really the main thing, to be honest. I mean, it dictates my whole mood, my whole sense of who I am on any given day. \n\nYou see, when I manage to stay small, when I feel neat and... and contained, like I was saying before, I just feel like I'm handling everything right in my life. It's like a barometer for me. If my work uniform fits loosely when I put it on for my evening shift—it's this blue button-down shirt and navy trousers, they issue them to us every two years, and I always make sure to ask for the exact same size—if it hangs just right on me, I feel accomplished. I feel like, okay, I've got my mother-in-law's meals sorted, I've got my husband's needs met, the house is clean, the offices will be spotless tonight, and I am completely in control of my world. \n\nBut... oh, doctor, if I catch a glimpse of myself in one of those big mirrors at work, or even just the small one above my bathroom sink at home, and my stomach looks the least bit full... it just ruins everything. I feel like I've completely let myself go. It doesn't matter if I spent four hours scrubbing baseboards at home or if I made the perfect pot roast for my family. If my stomach isn't perfectly flat, I feel like a total failure. I feel sloppy, like I'm not doing a good job at anything at all. \n\nMy husband, he'll tell me I'm doing a great job, he'll thank me for keeping the house running, but I can't even hear it. All I can think about is how I failed. Last Thursday, for instance, I felt just a tiny bit full—probably because I drank my coffee too fast before rushing out the door to catch my bus—and I spent half my evening shift just crying in the supply closet on the second floor. The second floor is usually quiet, mostly accounting firms, so nobody bothered me, but I just sat there among the floor wax and paper towels feeling like I was the most undisciplined, worthless person in the world just because of how my stomach looked. So, yes, it influences absolutely everything. If I'm not small and neat, then I just feel like everything else is falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives or diuretics, or exercise excessively to keep your weight down or prevent yourself from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. I've never done anything like that. Making myself sick... vomiting, I mean. No, I couldn't do that. It just seems so... well, working as a cleaner, you spend enough time cleaning up after other people who have been sick in the restrooms, especially in the winter months when the stomach bugs go around the offices. The last thing I'd want to do is make myself sick on purpose. \n\nAnd those pills, the laxatives and water pills, my mother-in-law takes them for her digestion and her blood pressure, and I see how she struggles with it, constantly needing to be near a bathroom. I just couldn't work like that. I can't be running to the restroom while I'm trying to finish my rounds. I have a very strict schedule, you see. I have to get through floors two, three, and four before 11 PM so the night manager can lock up the main stairwell, and I just don't have the time to be dealing with stomach cramps or emergencies from pills. \n\nAs for exercise... well, I don't go to a gym or run on a treadmill or anything like that. I mean, I'm 64, and I'm on my feet all evening! Pushing that heavy cart—it’s got the mop bucket full of water, the industrial trash bags, all the heavy cleaning fluids—it's quite a physical job on its own. Like I mentioned earlier, I might take the stairs instead of the elevator just to keep moving, or scrub the baseboards a little more vigorously if I'm feeling anxious about what I ate, but I wouldn't call it exercising excessively. I don't put on sweatpants and go jogging for hours. \n\nI just... I manage things by being very, very careful about what I put in my body in the first place. If I just don't eat the heavy things, if I just keep my meals very small and precise, then I don't have to worry about trying to get rid of it later. It's much neater that way, you know? Much more contained. I just focus on keeping my intake strictly controlled so I don't ever have to resort to anything messy or drastic like that.\n\nDoctor: Even though others have told you that you're too thin, do you still believe that you need to lose more weight or that you are overweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I know what Dr. Evans says, of course. He’s been my primary doctor for over a decade, since before my husband retired, and he keeps telling me I've gotten too thin. At my last check-up, he even seemed quite alarmed about it, which I thought was a bit much. And... well, I suppose I can see the physical evidence of it myself when I get dressed for my evening shift. I mentioned my work uniform earlier, those navy trousers they issue us? They are just hanging completely loose on me now. I have to wear a thick black belt pulled to the very tightest notch, and even then, I have to sort of fold the waistband over just a tiny bit in the back so it doesn't bunch up awkwardly under my blue button-down shirt. It's actually quite frustrating because it doesn't look as neat and tidy as I prefer to keep my appearance, but I absolutely refuse to put in a request with the night manager for a smaller size. \n\nBut... to answer your question, it’s a very strange thing. Because even with Dr. Evans fussing over my charts, and even with my work pants practically falling off me while I'm trying to empty the heavy recycling bins on the fourth floor, I still have this... this reaction. \n\nEvery morning, right after I wake up and before I even go to the kitchen to start the coffee for my husband and mother-in-law, I step on the scale in my bathroom. It's an older dial scale, the kind you have to tap with your toe to make sure the needle is perfectly resting on the zero before you step up, I've had it since the late nineties. And when I step on it, if that number is lower than it was the day before... I just feel this immense, overwhelming wave of relief. \n\nIt’s like a physical weight lifts off my chest. I don't necessarily look in the mirror and think, \"I am massively overweight,\" but I just feel like there's always room to be a little more precise. A little more whittled down. If the number goes down, it means I was successful the day before. It means I didn't let things get sloppy or out of hand. So, even though they all say I'm too thin, a very large part of me still feels like going lower is just... better. It feels safer. It’s like creating a little buffer for myself, just in case. So yes, I suppose I do still feel like I need to see that number keep going down, despite what the trousers or the doctors say.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your typical daily food intake in more detail—such as how many meals or snacks you have, how many calories you think you consume in a day, and whether you set strict rules or limits for yourself around eating?\n\nPatient: Well, my daily routine is very, very structured. I have to be, you see, with my husband being retired now and my mother-in-law living with us, not to mention my evening shifts. So, I do all the cooking. I actually really enjoy cooking for them, making sure they're taken care of. In the mornings, I get up early and I make a proper breakfast. Usually eggs, maybe some bacon or sausage for my husband, and a nice soft bowl of oatmeal for my mother-in-law because of her digestion—she needs things that are easy on the stomach. I cook these full, lovely plates for my husband and my mother-in-law, making sure everything is arranged just perfectly... but, well, by the time I actually sit down at the table with them, I usually tell myself that just my black coffee is enough for me. \n\nSometimes, if I feel I really need it, I might have half a piece of toast. Just half, mind you. I always cut it diagonally because it looks a bit neater that way, and I don't put any butter or jam on it, just plain, dry wheat toast. \n\nAs for the rest of the day, I don't really do snacks or anything like that. I'll prepare a nice lunch for them, maybe a sandwich or soup, and then I always make sure to prep their dinner before I have to leave for my shift at the offices at 4:30. I might make a casserole or a roast, something substantial. But for me? No, I don't eat those heavy things. While I'm chopping and prepping their meals, I might have a few vegetables... maybe three or four steamed broccoli florets or a couple of raw celery sticks. Just a few pieces. \n\nYou asked about calories and rules... I don't sit down with a calculator and add up the exact numbers, but my rule is just to keep things as minimal and precise as absolutely possible. I just tell myself that the coffee, a few vegetables, or that half a piece of toast is enough for me. It's plenty, really. If I ate the casseroles or the meats, I'd feel so sluggish and heavy. I'd feel it sitting in my stomach while I'm trying to push that heavy mop bucket around the second floor, and I just can't allow that. I have to stay light on my feet to get my floors done by 11 PM. So I just stick strictly to my coffee and my little bits of vegetables, and it just keeps everything so much neater. It just makes the whole day run smoother when I don't have to worry about feeling full or weighed down by all that food.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concerns about your weight or the way you control your eating get in the way of your daily life or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as getting in the way of my daily life... I mean, I still get all my work done. I am very, very diligent about my cleaning shifts. Like I was saying before, I get to the offices at 4:30 sharp every single evening, and I never miss a day. I push that heavy cart, I make sure the bathrooms are spotless, and I always, always finish floors two, three, and four before the night manager locks the stairwell at 11. So it certainly doesn't stop me from being productive or doing my job. I've always prided myself on my work ethic, and that hasn't changed one bit. \n\nBut... distress? Well, I suppose... I suppose it does cause a bit of a wrinkle when it comes to other things. It's the social things, mostly. Like the family dinners. I mentioned how I cook these full, lovely meals for my husband and my mother-in-law, making sure they have a nice roast or a comforting casserole. But... well, when it comes time to actually sit down at the dining table with them, I just can't really do it anymore. I used to sit with them, even if I was just having my black coffee or my three little broccoli florets, but lately, just being right there next to all that heavy food, smelling the gravy or the butter... it just makes me feel so incredibly anxious. My chest gets all tight. \n\nSo now, I usually find an excuse to stay in the kitchen while they eat. I'll wipe down the counters, which I've usually already done, or I'll start loading the dishwasher, or I'll reorganize the pantry shelves so all the soup cans are facing forward. I just have to keep moving and keep busy so I don't have to sit there at the table. \n\nAnd it’s not just at home, either. We used to be very involved with the Sunday potlucks at our church. We've gone to the same church for over thirty years, since before my husband retired. But I... I completely avoid the church meals now. You know how those church potlucks are, everyone brings their famous macaroni and cheese or their deep-dish pecan pies, and people get so terribly offended if you don't take a big, heaping scoop of what they made. It's just too much pressure. It's too chaotic, and I can't control or know what's in any of those dishes. So I've started making excuses. I'll tell them I have to leave right after the service to get ready for my shift, even though I don't work on Sunday evenings, or I'll say my mother-in-law's digestion is acting up and we need to get her home. \n\nIt actually really upset my husband the other day. We were driving back from the Sunday service, and he just looked over at me from the driver's seat... he looked so sad, honestly. He said to me, \"You know, you're around everyone, but you're not really joining in anymore.\" \n\nAnd that... well, that really stung. Because I am there! I'm doing the cooking, I'm taking care of the house, I'm going to the services. But he says I'm just physically in the room, hovering on the edges, without really participating in our life together. I guess... I guess when he puts it exactly like that, it does cause some distress. It makes me feel quite guilty. It's just that keeping my routine strictly controlled is the only way I can manage things, even if it means I can't sit at the table with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped having menstrual periods, or have they become very irregular?\n\nPatient: Oh, my periods? Well, goodness, doctor, I haven't had to worry about those for quite a long time now. I'm sixty-four, you see. I went through all of that... the change, I mean... back in my early fifties. \n\nIt was right around the time my husband was first talking about his retirement plans, and we were looking into getting the roof redone on the house. I remember it specifically because I was working at the old Miller building back then—they tore it down in 2015, which is such a shame because it had the most beautiful terrazzo floors that I took great pride in buffing—and it was just such a hassle dealing with my cycle at work. Toward the end of it all, things got so terribly unpredictable. You'd never know when it was going to happen, and trying to manage that while pushing a heavy cart around and trying to keep my uniform perfectly neat... well, it was just a nightmare. \n\nI like things to be precise and predictable, as I've mentioned. I like to know exactly what to expect from my day and my body. So when they finally stopped completely, oh, it must be well over ten years ago now, it was actually quite a relief. It's just one less mess to manage, one less thing to keep track of in my daily routine. So, no, they haven't stopped recently or anything like that, it's just simply my age. I suppose that's one blessing of getting older, isn't it? Everything is just... settled and finished in that regard, which is exactly how I prefer things to be. No surprises.\n\nDoctor: Even though you are underweight and others tell you this, do you still see yourself as needing to lose more weight or believe that you are overweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I know my husband tells me I’m too thin, and even my supervisor at the building, Mr. Henderson, he made a comment just the other day when I was picking up my supply keys from the front desk. He told me I looked like a strong breeze would blow me right over. But... I just don't see it the way they do at all. \n\nFor me, it's not so much about... well, I don't look in the mirror and think I'm some giant, lumbering person, not exactly. It’s more about the neatness of it all. Just like how I keep my floors, or how I organize the pantry shelves. When I stay small and neat, I just feel like I'm handling everything right. It tells me that I’m managing my life, my chores, my job, perfectly. \n\nI have this uniform for work, you see, a light blue button-down shirt and navy slacks. I wash and press it every single morning without fail, making sure the creases in the pants are sharp. And when I put it on, I need it to hang perfectly straight. If I look in the full-length mirror in our hallway before I leave for my shift, and my stomach looks the least bit full—even just the absolute slightest little curve pushing against the fabric of that blue shirt—I feel this awful, overwhelming wave of panic. I feel like I've completely let myself go. \n\nAnd I just cannot abide that. I really can't. It’s just so terribly important to me that everything stays perfectly in its place, and that has to include me, too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If the number goes down, it means I was successful the day before. It means I didn't let things get sloppy or out of hand. So, even though they all say I'm too thin, a very large part of me still feels like going lower is just... better. It feels safer. It’s like creating a little buffer for myself, just in case. So yes, I suppose I do still feel like I need to see that number keep going down, despite what the trousers or the doctors say.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your typical daily food intake in more detail—such as how many meals or snacks you have, how many calories you think you consume in a day, and whether you set strict rules or limits for yourself around eating?\n\nPatient: Well, my daily routine is very, very structured. I have to be, you see, with my husband being retired now and my mother-in-law living with us, not to mention my evening shifts. So, I do all the cooking. I actually really enjoy cooking for them, making sure they're taken care of. In the mornings, I get up early and I make a proper breakfast. Usually eggs, maybe some bacon or sausage for my husband, and a nice soft bowl of oatmeal for my mother-in-law because of her digestion—she needs things that are easy on the stomach. I cook these full, lovely plates for my husband and my mother-in-law, making sure everything is arranged just perfectly... but, well, by the time I actually sit down at the table with them, I usually tell myself that just my black coffee is enough for me. \n\nSometimes, if I feel I really need it, I might have half a piece of toast. Just half, mind you. I always cut it diagonally because it looks a bit neater that way, and I don't put any butter or jam on it, just plain, dry wheat toast. \n\nAs for the rest of the day, I don't really do snacks or anything like that. I'll prepare a nice lunch for them, maybe a sandwich or soup, and then I always make sure to prep their dinner before I have to leave for my shift at the offices at 4:30. I might make a casserole or a roast, something substantial. But for me? No, I don't eat those heavy things. While I'm chopping and prepping their meals, I might have a few vegetables... maybe three or four steamed broccoli florets or a couple of raw celery sticks. Just a few pieces. \n\nYou asked about calories and rules... I don't sit down with a calculator and add up the exact numbers, but my rule is just to keep things as minimal and precise as absolutely possible. I just tell myself that the coffee, a few vegetables, or that half a piece of toast is enough for me. It's plenty, really. If I ate the casseroles or the meats, I'd feel so sluggish and heavy. I'd feel it sitting in my stomach while I'm trying to push that heavy mop bucket around the second floor, and I just can't allow that. I have to stay light on my feet to get my floors done by 11 PM. So I just stick strictly to my coffee and my little bits of vegetables, and it just keeps everything so much neater. It just makes the whole day run smoother when I don't have to worry about feeling full or weighed down by all that food.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concerns about your weight or the way you control your eating get in the way of your daily life or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as getting in the way of my daily life... I mean, I still get all my work done. I am very, very diligent about my cleaning shifts. Like I was saying before, I get to the offices at 4:30 sharp every single evening, and I never miss a day. I push that heavy cart, I make sure the bathrooms are spotless, and I always, always finish floors two, three, and four before the night manager locks the stairwell at 11. So it certainly doesn't stop me from being productive or doing my job. I've always prided myself on my work ethic, and that hasn't changed one bit. \n\nBut... distress? Well, I suppose... I suppose it does cause a bit of a wrinkle when it comes to other things. It's the social things, mostly. Like the family dinners. I mentioned how I cook these full, lovely meals for my husband and my mother-in-law, making sure they have a nice roast or a comforting casserole. But... well, when it comes time to actually sit down at the dining table with them, I just can't really do it anymore. I used to sit with them, even if I was just having my black coffee or my three little broccoli florets, but lately, just being right there next to all that heavy food, smelling the gravy or the butter... it just makes me feel so incredibly anxious. My chest gets all tight. \n\nSo now, I usually find an excuse to stay in the kitchen while they eat. I'll wipe down the counters, which I've usually already done, or I'll start loading the dishwasher, or I'll reorganize the pantry shelves so all the soup cans are facing forward. I just have to keep moving and keep busy so I don't have to sit there at the table. \n\nAnd it’s not just at home, either. We used to be very involved with the Sunday potlucks at our church. We've gone to the same church for over thirty years, since before my husband retired. But I... I completely avoid the church meals now. You know how those church potlucks are, everyone brings their famous macaroni and cheese or their deep-dish pecan pies, and people get so terribly offended if you don't take a big, heaping scoop of what they made. It's just too much pressure. It's too chaotic, and I can't control or know what's in any of those dishes. So I've started making excuses. I'll tell them I have to leave right after the service to get ready for my shift, even though I don't work on Sunday evenings, or I'll say my mother-in-law's digestion is acting up and we need to get her home. \n\nIt actually really upset my husband the other day. We were driving back from the Sunday service, and he just looked over at me from the driver's seat... he looked so sad, honestly. He said to me, \"You know, you're around everyone, but you're not really joining in anymore.\" \n\nAnd that... well, that really stung. Because I am there! I'm doing the cooking, I'm taking care of the house, I'm going to the services. But he says I'm just physically in the room, hovering on the edges, without really participating in our life together. I guess... I guess when he puts it exactly like that, it does cause some distress. It makes me feel quite guilty. It's just that keeping my routine strictly controlled is the only way I can manage things, even if it means I can't sit at the table with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped having menstrual periods, or have they become very irregular?\n\nPatient: Oh, my periods? Well, goodness, doctor, I haven't had to worry about those for quite a long time now. I'm sixty-four, you see. I went through all of that... the change, I mean... back in my early fifties. \n\nIt was right around the time my husband was first talking about his retirement plans, and we were looking into getting the roof redone on the house. I remember it specifically because I was working at the old Miller building back then—they tore it down in 2015, which is such a shame because it had the most beautiful terrazzo floors that I took great pride in buffing—and it was just such a hassle dealing with my cycle at work. Toward the end of it all, things got so terribly unpredictable. You'd never know when it was going to happen, and trying to manage that while pushing a heavy cart around and trying to keep my uniform perfectly neat... well, it was just a nightmare. \n\nI like things to be precise and predictable, as I've mentioned. I like to know exactly what to expect from my day and my body. So when they finally stopped completely, oh, it must be well over ten years ago now, it was actually quite a relief. It's just one less mess to manage, one less thing to keep track of in my daily routine. So, no, they haven't stopped recently or anything like that, it's just simply my age. I suppose that's one blessing of getting older, isn't it? Everything is just... settled and finished in that regard, which is exactly how I prefer things to be. No surprises.\n\nDoctor: Even though you are underweight and others tell you this, do you still see yourself as needing to lose more weight or believe that you are overweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I know my husband tells me I’m too thin, and even my supervisor at the building, Mr. Henderson, he made a comment just the other day when I was picking up my supply keys from the front desk. He told me I looked like a strong breeze would blow me right over. But... I just don't see it the way they do at all. \n\nFor me, it's not so much about... well, I don't look in the mirror and think I'm some giant, lumbering person, not exactly. It’s more about the neatness of it all. Just like how I keep my floors, or how I organize the pantry shelves. When I stay small and neat, I just feel like I'm handling everything right. It tells me that I’m managing my life, my chores, my job, perfectly. \n\nI have this uniform for work, you see, a light blue button-down shirt and navy slacks. I wash and press it every single morning without fail, making sure the creases in the pants are sharp. And when I put it on, I need it to hang perfectly straight. If I look in the full-length mirror in our hallway before I leave for my shift, and my stomach looks the least bit full—even just the absolute slightest little curve pushing against the fabric of that blue shirt—I feel this awful, overwhelming wave of panic. I feel like I've completely let myself go. \n\nAnd I just cannot abide that. I really can't. If my stomach isn't perfectly flat, if I feel like I'm taking up too much space... it just feels so sloppy. It feels like I'm failing at my duties, like I'm losing my grip on everything I'm supposed to be managing. You can't be a proper caretaker for your family and a diligent worker if you're letting yourself go and getting all soft and undisciplined. So, yes, I suppose I do always feel the need to keep trimming down, or at least to be extremely careful, just to make absolutely sure I stay small. It’s just so terribly important to me that everything stays perfectly in its place, and that has to include me, too."
